# The hallmarks of protein and amino acid restriction in aging and longevity

[Bailey A. Knopf](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#)1,2,3 ∙ [Dudley W. Lamming](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#)1,2,3,4,5 [Send email to dlamming@medicine.wisc.edu](mailto:dlamming@medicine.wisc.edu)

## Summary

Despite widespread recommendations for higher protein intake during aging, increasing evidence suggests that dietary protein restriction (PR) promotes metabolic health, healthspan, and lifespan across diverse organisms. Mechanistic studies further demonstrate that restriction of specific amino acids, particularly methionine, isoleucine, and valine, recapitulates many of the benefits of PR, highlighting the specific amino acid composition of the diet as a key determinant of aging. Here, we define the hallmarks of PR through the lens of aging, examining how PR and restriction of specific essential amino acids impact metabolic health, nutrient sensing, senescence, mitochondrial function, and the epigenome. We also discuss the known and unknown roles of non-essential amino acids in healthy aging. Overall, this review provides a comprehensive overview of current knowledge regarding the benefits of PR and amino acid restriction for healthy aging and highlights the therapeutic potential of interventions based on these diets to promote healthspan and longevity.

## Introduction

Since McCay’s 1935 report that calorie restriction (CR) extends rat lifespan, CR has proven to be a robust and reproducible intervention, with benefits for lifespan and healthspan in organisms ranging from yeast to non-human primates, as well as significant benefits for human health in short-term studies.[1](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) However, a CR diet is generally thought to be too difficult for many people,[2](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) which raises the question: is there a way to achieve the healthspan and lifespan effects of CR without reducing overall energy intake?

In rodents, the ratio of dietary macronutrients profoundly impacts lifespan, with low-protein, high-carbohydrate diets extending lifespan and improving metabolic health.[3](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[4](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) A low-protein diet, also referred to as protein restriction (PR), is a robust geroprotective regimen that lowers total dietary protein intake while still meeting nutritional needs. Like CR, PR improves healthspan and increases the lifespan of yeast, flies, and rodents.[3](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[5](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[6](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[7](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[8](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[9](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#)

Despite these findings, human dietary recommendations generally suggest increasing protein intake. While the official Recommended Dietary Allowance (RDA) for protein is 0.8 g/kg of bodyweight regardless of sex or age,[10](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) intakes of 1.0–1.2 g protein/kg of bodyweight are routinely recommended for individuals over the age of 65 to prevent sarcopenia and frailty,[11](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[12](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[13](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[14](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[15](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) and the most recent Dietary Guidelines for America suggest 1.2–1.6 g protein/kg of body weight.[16](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) These recommendations are supported by studies finding that short-term high protein diets promote weight loss, largely by promoting satiety and reducing food intake[17](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[18](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[19](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[20](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) in highly compliant subjects.[21](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Sarcopenia, age-related muscle loss, is of great concern in the elderly due to its contribution to frailty, and increasing dietary protein, often paired with resistance exercise, is advised by medical doctors as a way to improve muscle mass, combat sarcopenia, and reduce frailty in older individuals.[12](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[22](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[23](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[24](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#)

However, accumulating evidence challenges the idea that higher protein intake is beneficial. Human association studies have found that high-protein diets are associated with an increased risk of diabetes, cancer, and mortality,[25](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[26](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) as well as an increased risk of death due to cardiovascular events.[27](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[28](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) An analysis of the National Health and Nutrition Examination Survey (NHANES) data found that higher protein consumption correlates with increased mortality and age-associated disease incidence, including diabetes.[25](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[26](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[29](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) A 2023 study in the UK reported that increased dietary protein intake was positively associated with sarcopenia in older twins, challenging the conventional idea that dietary protein prevents sarcopenia.[30](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[31](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[32](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[33](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#)

Randomized controlled trials (RCTs) support the metabolic benefits of PR in humans. In 2016, we reported that individuals consuming a low-protein diet for 43 days exhibited decreased body weight and fat mass and reduced fasting blood glucose despite increased caloric intake, mirroring our findings in rodents.[34](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) A recent study in lean men found that a 5-week PR intervention improves insulin sensitivity and increases energy expenditure.[35](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) In the context of diabetes, short-term (27 days) PR was shown to reduce adiposity, improve insulin sensitivity, decrease circulating glucose and lipid levels, and reduce overall inflammation in patients with metabolic syndrome.[36](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Additional studies have found that PR lowers blood levels of insulin and hemoglobin A1C and improves kidney disease outcomes in diabetic patients.[37](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Conversely, a high-protein, low-carbohydrate diet was recently shown to be less effective than a moderate-protein diet in lowering blood glucose, improving glucose tolerance, and promoting prediabetes remission.[38](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#)

These seemingly contradictory studies leave many wondering what the optimal amount of dietary protein is for healthy aging. A 2025 report found that 61% of American consumers increased their protein intake in 2024, up from 48% in 2019, suggesting that many Americans believe that higher protein intake is beneficial.[39](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Food manufacturers have tapped into this belief by increasing production of protein-dense foods.[40](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) As protein-fortified foods flood grocery store shelves and older adults are urged to increase protein intake, the need to understand how dietary protein impacts aging is more important than ever. In this review, we will discuss the hallmarks of PR in the context of aging and age-associated diseases, the effects of individual amino acids, and the molecular mechanisms underlying these effects and highlight gaps in our knowledge and priorities for future research.

## Hallmarks of dietary PR

Conducting studies in model organisms allows precise control of nutrition, with respect to both quantity and composition. Recent work from our lab and others has shown that PR extends the lifespan of rodents.[3](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[5](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[41](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[42](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[43](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) [Table 1](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#tbl1) summarizes the broadly positive impact of PR on the lifespan of laboratory animals. Using laboratory models as a basis for PR response, we will discuss the hallmarks of PR, shown in [Figure 1](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#fig1), through an aging-focused lens.

[![](https://www.cell.com/cms/10.1016/j.cpblue.2026.100079/asset/a2b2d67d-26b5-4096-ba91-6de0bf266e5c/main.assets/gr1.jpg)](https://www.cell.com/cms/10.1016/j.cpblue.2026.100079/asset/10e75521-5e62-4f5a-89f5-4ed4f6a20a0f/main.assets/gr1_lrg.jpg "View full size image in a new tab")

### Hallmark 1: Metabolic health

Aging in laboratory animals and humans is characterized by decreased energy expenditure,[52](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[53](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[54](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) increased body weight, and fat accumulation.[55](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) In young rodents, dietary PR improves metabolic function by reducing adiposity, increasing energy expenditure, and improving glucose homeostasis.[5](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[56](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[57](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[58](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[59](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[60](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) This is not due to decreased calorie intake; PR actually increases food intake yet decreases fat mass and weight due to increased energy expenditure.[57](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[61](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[62](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Although these effects are most pronounced in males, females also show improved metabolic health with PR.[63](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[64](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) PR remains effective when initiated late in life (18–20 months of age), increasing energy expenditure and improving metabolic health in aged mice.[65](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[66](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) The metabolic improvements induced by PR likely contribute to the longevity benefits of PR, making improved metabolic health our first hallmark.

A primary mediator of the metabolic benefits of PR is fibroblast growth factor 21 (FGF21), a hormone induced by nutritional stress, including fasting.[67](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Dietary PR robustly elevates FGF21 in mice, rats, and humans.[34](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[35](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[57](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[68](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Multiple groups have shown that FGF21 is required for many metabolic effects of PR, including increased food intake, reduced body weight, increased energy expenditure, and extended lifespan.[41](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[57](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[64](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[69](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[70](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Other geroprotective diets, including CR,[71](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) methionine restriction,[72](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) branched-chain amino acid (BCAA) restriction,[56](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) and isoleucine restriction,[73](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) induce FGF21, and at least some of the metabolic benefits of these diets may be mediated by FGF21.

Many of the functions of FGF21 are mediated by adipose tissue. FGF21 promotes energy expenditure in part through uncoupling protein 1 (UCP1)-dependent thermogenic beiging of white adipose tissue (WAT).[74](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[75](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) PR stimulates WAT beiging via the action of FGF21 in the brain, where FGF21 stimulates sympathetic signaling.[76](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[77](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[78](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[79](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) FGF21 may also be able to directly stimulate WAT thermogenesis in the absence of UCP1.[80](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[81](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) PR similarly increases thermogenesis in brown adipose tissue (BAT) via an FGF21-dependent mechanism, promoting glucose consumption via sympathetic signaling, suggesting a systemic adipose tissue response to FGF21.[78](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[82](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Although FGF21 promotes health via BAT-independent mechanisms,[83](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) the increased activity of BAT may also contribute to healthy aging. The increased energy expenditure and thermogenesis may drive increased food intake with dietary PR through shifting brain signaling toward protein appetite, as mice administered exogenous FGF21 exhibited a strong behavioral preference for protein-rich food.[57](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[68](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[84](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[85](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#)

Beyond thermogenesis, FGF21 is also a key regulator of lipid homeostasis. PR reduces circulating triglycerides by increasing disposal and decreasing production upon FGF21 induction.[86](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) FGF21 stimulates fatty acid uptake by adipocytes, most likely by increasing insulin sensitivity.[86](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[87](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) FGF21 also mediates the effect of PR on cell senescence and inflammation in WAT.[88](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) In obese rodent models, exogenous FGF21 reduces hepatic steatosis, inflammation, and fibrosis.[89](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[90](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[91](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Administration of FGF21 to obese mice improves kidney pathology through decreased renal damage, renal lipid accumulation, inflammation, and fibrosis.[92](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[93](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) These data suggest that FGF21 administration could be leveraged to treat age-related metabolic diseases.

FGF21 plays a key role in regulating lifespan. Mice overexpressing _Fgf21_ have a significant increase in lifespan (∼30% in males and ∼40% in females), implicating FGF21 as a geroprotective hormone.[94](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Critically, _Fgf21-_null mice do not live longer on PR, identifying FGF21 as essential for PR’s longevity benefits.[41](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) While circulating FGF21 is produced primarily by the liver, adipocyte-specific _Fgf21_ overexpression increases plasma FGF21 and significantly increases the median survival of high-fat-fed male mice.[69](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) _Fgf21_ overexpression in the adipose tissue and thymus also slows immune aging, which may contribute to the long life of mice overexpressing _Fgf21_.[95](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#)

### Hallmark 2: Nutrient-sensing pathways

A leading hypothesis for the geroprotective effects of PR is altered nutrient pathway signaling. Amino acid sensing is physiologically important because scarcity promotes catabolic pathways and induces autophagy. Molecular analysis of aging rate indicators shows that PR shifts the molecular signature of the liver toward a more youthful state.[66](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) General control nonderepressible 2 (GCN2) and mechanistic target of rapamycin (mTOR) are two kinases we will discuss, which phosphorylate a subset of aging rate indicators. The ability for organisms to muster a coordinated response to PR requires activation of GCN2 and inhibition of mTORC1.

#### Pathway 1: GCN2

GCN2 is an evolutionarily conserved stress-responsive serine/threonine kinase that plays a key role in maintaining cellular homeostasis.[96](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) GCN2 is activated by amino acid restriction through uncharged tRNAs, which bind to GCN2 and directly activate it,[97](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[98](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) as well as by ribosomes stalling and colliding on RNA.[99](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Activated GCN2 phosphorylates the alpha subunit of eukaryotic initiation factor 2 (eIF2α), suppressing global protein synthesis during nutrient stress[100](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[101](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) while selectively promoting the translation of activating transcription factor 4 (ATF4), a key activator of stress response and autophagy genes.[102](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#)

_Drosophila_ lacking _Gcn2_ die when amino acids are restricted,[103](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) while deletion of _Gcn2_ in yeast blocks the ability of dietary restriction to extend lifespan,[104](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) demonstrating the importance of _Gcn2_ across species. Conversely, overexpression of _Gcn2_ in yeast and _Drosophila_ extends lifespan.[105](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[106](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) During PR, GCN2 is initially required for the induction of FGF21,[68](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[107](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) but when PR is extended, FGF21 is induced even in _Gcn2_-deficient mice. The response to PR is coordinated by GCN2 activation of FGF21 and by inhibition of mTORC1, thereby activating autophagy pathways.[108](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Similarly, GCN2 activates hepatic gluconeogenesis in response to decreased amino acid availability, suggesting its pivotal role in organism metabolism.[109](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#)

#### Pathway 2: mTOR

The master nutrient sensor mTOR forms two complexes: mTOR complex 1 (mTORC1) and complex 2 (mTORC2), with distinctive functions and substrates and which impact aging in different ways. Pharmacological or genetic inhibition of mTORC1 extends lifespan in many species; in mice, inhibition of mTORC2 worsens insulin sensitivity, whereas in _Drosophila_, _C. elegans_, and mice, it decreases lifespan.[110](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[111](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[112](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[113](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[114](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[115](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Normal aging leads to constitutive mTORC1 activation in many tissues, leading to cellular dysfunction,[116](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[117](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[118](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) and mouse knockout models have revealed that without inhibition of mTORC1, the benefits of PR are lost.[119](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) PR inhibits mTORC1 in a variety of tissues, including liver, heart, muscle, adipose tissue, and brain.[3](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[66](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[120](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[121](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#)

mTORC1 integrates nutrient availability and hormonal cues to regulate cellular processes. The canonical regulation of mTORC1 by amino acids has been reviewed at length.[122](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[123](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Most recently, it has been discovered that internal and exogenous sources of amino acids differentially regulate mTORC1[124](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) and that leucine signals to mTORC1 in specific neurons by binding to and activating a specific voltage-gated calcium channel.[125](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) PR likely inhibits mTORC1 through multiple complementary mechanisms, including reduced availability of mTORC1-activating amino acids and alterations in hormones.

PR may promote autophagy while inhibiting protein synthesis. Autophagy preserves cellular homeostasis by removing “cellular trash,” including protein aggregates and dysfunctional organelles.[126](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[127](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Upon mTORC1 inhibition, ULK1 initiates autophagosome formation; mice lacking the ability to activate hepatic autophagy have blunted responses to PR.[126](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Many age-associated diseases exhibit impaired autophagy, including neurodegeneration,[128](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) whereas fasting and geroprotective interventions tend to activate autophagy.[129](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Inhibition of mTORC1 suppresses translation by reducing phosphorylation of S6K1 and ribosome biogenesis. Genetic ablation of _S6K1_ in mice results in a significant extension of lifespan,[130](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) suggesting that PR might promote lifespan by decreasing S6K1 activity. Decreased mTORC1 activity also inactivates translation by phosphorylating eIF4E-binding protein 1 (4E-BP1), blocking eIF4E from binding to RNA and initiating translation. Together, these coordinated effects on autophagy and protein synthesis may drive many of the geroprotective effects of dietary PR.

### Hallmark 3: Senescence

Cellular senescence is an irreversible cell-cycle arrest triggered by different cellular stressors.[131](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[132](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Senescent cells develop a senescence-associated secretory phenotype (SASP) characterized by the activation of pro-inflammatory pathways that promote tissue dysfunction, which can accelerate aging,[133](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) while clearance of senescent cells can ameliorate age-related disease.[134](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#)

High-protein diets increase senescence in the liver, while PR reduces hepatic senescence.[66](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[135](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Similarly, high-protein diets increase inflammation and senescence in WAT,[136](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) while PR reduces both senescence and inflammation in the adipose tissue through an FGF21-dependent mechanism.[88](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) High-protein diets also promote senescent cell accumulation in the kidney, and PR reduces this burden.[137](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#)

FGF21 also suppresses senescence in cultured cells, reducing drug-induced senescence and the expression of pro-inflammatory SASP markers.[138](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[139](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) In diabetic mice, FGF21 administration reduced senescent cells in vascular smooth muscle cells, which experience exacerbated senescence due to chronic hyperglycemia.[140](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) The detrimental nature of senescent cells in the metabolic tissues could contribute to worsening obesity upon aging, and the ability of dietary PR to decrease adipose and liver senescence may represent an important mechanism by which PR regulates healthspan.

### Hallmark 4: Mitochondrial dysfunction

Possessing their own genome, metabolome, proteome, and transcriptome, mitochondria generate ATP while also serving as central regulators of metabolism. Mitochondria become less efficient with age, with reduced mitochondria recycling, the accumulation of DNA damage, and impaired electron transport chain activity reducing ATP production. Mitochondria are the primary source of reactive oxygen species (ROS), extremely reactive chemical molecules that damage proteins, lipids, and DNA. Increased ROS with aging can drive senescence in a variety of cell types,[134](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) and mitochondrial dysfunction has been linked to many age-associated diseases, including insulin resistance and type 2 diabetes (T2D).[141](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#)

While CR preserves mitochondrial function by reducing ROS, improving mitochondrial biogenesis, and reducing DNA mutations, few studies have examined the impact of PR on mitochondria.[142](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[143](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) PR reduces hepatic ROS and increases mitochondrial activity, suggesting that PR improves mitochondrial function.[3](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[144](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[145](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Conversely, feeding mice a high-protein diet significantly reduces electron transport chain activity in the skeletal muscle and impairs exercise endurance, suggesting that increased dietary protein may be harmful to skeletal muscle mitochondrial homeostasis, perhaps due to mTORC1-mediated suppression of AMP-activated protein kinase (AMPK).[146](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[147](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Similarly, increased dietary protein also decreases hepatic ATP synthesis in chickens.[148](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#)

The association of PR with improved mitochondrial function and high-protein diets with dysfunction is not uniformly observed. Dietary protein supplements have been reported to increase mitochondrial respiration and reduce oxidative stress in the brain,[149](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) while a different clinical trial reported no improvements in mitochondrial energy production after 10 days of PR.[150](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) In a mouse study, we observed no effect of dietary protein on mitochondrial function in skeletal muscle.[151](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) These varying effects of PR on mitochondrial function may be due to experimental factors that vary between experiments. These studies highlight not only the potential benefits of PR for mitochondrial function but also the remaining knowledge gap in clarifying how dietary protein regulates mitochondrial function.

### Hallmark 5: Epigenetic modification

Epigenetic modifications, such as DNA methylation and posttranslational modifications (PTMs) of histone tails, allow organisms to adjust gene expression without altering the underlying DNA. Aging is accompanied by and perhaps driven in part by the erosion of the epigenetic landscape, leading to improper gene regulation and promoting the hallmarks of aging, including cell senescence.[152](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Histone methylation, acetylation, ubiquitination, and phosphorylation all change with age, and a subset of histone PTMs can be used as molecular markers of cellular aging.[152](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[153](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#)

The severe food restriction experienced during the Dutch winter of 1944/1945 resulted in transgenerational effects of nutrition on health mediated by epigenetic mechanisms,[154](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[155](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) and CR has been shown to blunt age-associated changes in DNA methylation.[156](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) In contrast, little is known about how PR impacts the epigenome. PR in pregnant rodents leads to loci-specific changes in histone methylation and thus gene expression,[157](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[158](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) while in pigs, changing dietary protein levels led to altered expression of DNA methyltransferases in the liver and skeletal muscle in the mothers,[159](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) as well as loci-specific changes in histone methylation in the livers of the offspring.[160](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Some studies report that the offspring from PR mothers exhibit reduced body weight, reduced hepatic lipid accumulation, and improved glucose tolerance early in life,[161](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) while others report long-term metabolic dysfunction in the offspring.[162](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[163](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[164](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) As discussed below, accumulating evidence suggests that dietary levels of specific amino acids regulate histone PTMs.[165](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[166](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[167](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Collectively, these data suggest that PR can impact gene expression in both organisms and their offspring by regulating the epigenome and may promote healthspan by preserving or restoring a youthful epigenetic landscape.

### Hallmark 6: Healthy aging

PR not only extends lifespan but also extends healthspan across diverse species.[3](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[5](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[41](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[49](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[168](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[169](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[170](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[171](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[172](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[173](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) In addition to the many positive effects on the hallmarks we have defined above, a key factor in healthspan is frailty, a state of vulnerability in which the risk of dependency, falls, hospitalization, and mortality increases.

Older adults are now advised to consume more protein to prevent frailty and combat sarcopenia, a recommendation supported by several studies. For example, adults consuming 1.2 g/kg of bodyweight/day had 40% less skeletal muscle loss over 3 years compared to those consuming 0.8 g/kg.[14](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Higher protein intake is associated with a reduced risk of frailty in observational studies,[174](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[175](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) and an analysis of UK Biobank data found that consuming more protein reduced the risk of frailty in individuals over the age of 50.[176](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) However, a recent study of British twins showed that eating more protein is associated with increased risk of sarcopenia,[30](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) while consumption of a low-protein Mediterranean diet is associated with increased muscle mass and strength.[177](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Protein source may partially explain these conflicting data, as plant but not animal protein has been shown to be associated with a reduced risk of frailty.[178](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#)

Our own studies have found that although a high-protein diet promotes muscle growth, especially when combined with resistance exercise,[151](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) PR blunts age-associated increases in frailty.[173](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Further, PR initiated late in life reduces lean mass but may reduce or blunt further declines in frailty.[64](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[66](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Exercise can largely offset the lean mass loss associated with low-protein diets in humans, suggesting that individuals may be able to obtain the benefits of PR while preserving muscle.[179](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#)

Although the effects of PR on human lifespan are unknown, it has been hypothesized that the long lifespan of the Japanese population of Okinawa is due to their diet, which contains about 9% protein and is strikingly similar to that of PR laboratory studies.[180](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) The Okinawan diet also derives 80% of its calories from plant sources, and individuals generally consume fewer calories overall, making it difficult to assign all of the benefits to the lower protein intake.[181](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Nevertheless, these observations support the possibility that PR may promote healthy aging in humans.

Another important component of aging is resilience, the ability to “bounce back” from stress. Although lab animals are maintained in a low-stress, controlled environment, the ability to withstand and recover from a variety of stresses, including extremes of temperature, fasting, and infections, is critical to healthy aging in humans. Cellular resilience, the ability of cells to respond to stressors such as hypoxia, pathogens, or heat, also declines with age.[182](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) The loss of resilience contributes to chronic disease, multimorbidity, and death and is influenced by diet, lifestyle, and genetic factors. PR in _Drosophila_ increases resilience during aging, improving responses to starvation, bacterial infection, and heat stress.[169](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) The impact of PR on resilience in mammals remains to be determined.

## Are there distinct roles for every amino acid?

### Essential amino acids

There have long been questions about the role of different dietary amino acids in the benefits of CR and PR. Restriction of the essential amino acids (EAAs) recapitulates the benefits of PR on lifespan in both _Drosophila_ and mice, while restriction of the non-EAAs (NEAAs) has no benefit for lifespan.[49](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[183](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Among the EAAs, methionine and the BCAAs—leucine, isoleucine, and valine—are likely the primary drivers of these effects, as restriction of these individual amino acids has benefits for both metabolic health and longevity in rodents.[73](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[173](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[184](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[185](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[186](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Several additional EAAs and NEAAs may also contribute to distinct aspects of the response to PR. These studies are summarized below and in [Table 2](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#tbl2).

|Amino acid|Degree of restriction|Model|Effects on hallmark|Effect on lifespan|Citations|
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
|BCAAs|66%|C57BL/6J male mice|improved metabolic health, decreased mTORC1 activity reduced frailty|30% increase|Richardson et al.[5](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#)|
|85%|Dahomey flies|decreased mTORC1 activity|∼65% increase|Juricic et al.[187](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#)|
|Methionine|65%–77%|CB6F1 female mice|improved metabolic health|∼10% increase|Miller et al.[184](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#)|
|93%|CB6F1 male mice|–|7% increase|Sun et al.[188](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#)|
|80%|Fisher 344 male rats|–|30% increase, 42% increase|Orentreich et al.[186](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#); Richie et al.[189](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#)|
|Isoleucine|66%|UM-HET3 male mice|improved metabolic health, reduced SASP, reduced frailty|33% increase|Green et al.[173](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#)|
|0% transiently|Dahomey flies|–|∼5% increase|Fulton et al.[190](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#)|
|Valine|66%|C57BL/6J male mice|improved metabolic health, decreased senescence, reduced frailty|23% increase|Calubag et al.[185](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#)|

Current studies investigating the impact of essential amino acid restriction on the hallmarks of protein restriction and lifespan of laboratory organisms

### Methionine

Methionine restriction extends the lifespan of many species.[183](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[184](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[186](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[191](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[192](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[193](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) When initiated early or late in life, methionine restriction reduces body weight; increases lean mass; improves mitochondrial function, lipid metabolism, and frailty; and extends the lifespan of rodents.[184](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[194](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[195](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[196](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[197](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[198](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) The metabolic response to methionine restriction is partially dependent on FGF21, and mice lacking _Fgf21_ exhibit blunted responses of energy expenditure, beiging, and insulin sensitivity upon methionine restriction.[72](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[199](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Methionine restriction also inhibits mTORC1, which may contribute to the lifespan-extending effects.[196](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#)

Methionine is the precursor of S-adenoysl-L-methionine, the universal methyl donor.[200](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Dietary methionine deprivation may impact physiology through changes in histone methylation due to decreased availability of methyl donors,[201](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) suggesting that chromatin alterations are a direct result of carbon sensing and availability. While this could be thought to be detrimental to cellular health, cells are able to preserve heterochromatic stability through upregulation of H3K9 mono-methylation in the liver, suggesting that organisms may undergo epigenetic adaptation in response to altered amino acid availability.[166](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[167](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#)

There are promising studies investigating methionine restriction or sulfur amino acid restriction in humans. Eight weeks of sulfur amino acid restriction increased FGF21, reduced leptin, and reduced body weight.[202](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[203](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Likewise, individuals adhering to a strict vegan diet, which has been shown to reduce blood levels of methionine,[204](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[205](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) experience weight loss, reduced circulating lipid levels, and improved glucose homeostasis.[206](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[207](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[208](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#)

### BCAAs

High circulating levels of BCAAs are associated with obesity, insulin resistance, and mortality in humans.[209](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[210](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[211](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) In rodents, high-BCAA diets promote obesity and insulin resistance, increase inflammation, and decrease lifespan.[209](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[212](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[213](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[214](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Conversely, dietary restriction of the BCAAs promotes metabolic health in both lean and obese rodents, and in lean mice, it reduces frailty and extends lifespan, recapitulating many of the benefits of PR.[5](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[34](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[56](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[59](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[73](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[215](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[216](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[217](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) BCAA restriction improves Alzheimer’s disease (AD) pathology and cognition in rodent models of AD, and restriction of each individual BCAA impacts cognition, Aβ plaque deposition, and tau pathology differently. While these studies generally conclude that decreased dietary BCAAs are beneficial for healthy aging, positive effects of BCAAs on mitochondrial function, hepatic steatosis, physical performance, and lifespan have been reported.[218](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[219](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[220](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[221](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#)

BCAA restriction also recapitulates several of the hallmarks of PR. BCAA restriction reduces mTORC1 signaling, induces _Fgf21_ expression, and reduces hepatic cellular senescence.[5](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[56](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[222](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) BCAA restriction in individuals with metabolic syndrome resulted in improved mitochondrial respiratory efficiency, promoting overall health.[223](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) In _Drosophila,_ epigenetic changes induced by BCAA restriction mediate the benefits of this diet for lifespan.[165](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#)

To date, there have been two human studies investigating the impact of dietary BCAA restriction. The first reduced BCAA intake by 75% for 7 days in lean individuals and found that circulating BCAAs were reduced by 50% and insulin sensitivity was improved.[224](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) The second RCT reduced dietary BCAAs by ∼60% for 4 weeks, resulting in improved glucose and insulin homeostasis, increased circulating FGF21, and reduced mTORC1 signaling in the WAT.[223](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Conversely, increased BCAA intake was correlated with reduced sarcopenia risk in a cohort of Chinese adults aged 55+,[225](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) while a study found that BCAA supplementation paired with light resistance exercise improved muscle strength in patients in long-term care.[226](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) The incomplete consensus on BCAA restriction/supplementation, perhaps due to alternative factors such as physical activity, highlights the need for more research to understand the impact of each on health and aging. While the BCAAs are most often considered as a group, recent investigations have shown that each individual BCAA plays an important role in aging.

#### BCAA: Leucine

Leucine is a potent mTORC1 activator that stimulates skeletal muscle anabolism[227](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) and has been rigorously studied as a dietary intervention to promote healthy aging. In humans, leucine supplementation increases muscle protein synthesis[227](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[228](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) but does not improve muscle function.[229](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[230](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Leucine supplementation decreases body fat in aging rats,[231](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) consistent with our finding that leucine restriction in mice increases body weight, adiposity, and fat mass.[34](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) However, some groups have reported the opposite, finding that leucine restriction is effective at reducing fat mass and improving glucose homeostasis.[196](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[232](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) It is possible that differences in the specific degree of leucine restriction, or the ratio of other amino acids to leucine, explain these divergent results.

Similarly, divergent results about the effect of leucine on cardiovascular health have been reported. Some studies suggest that leucine negatively impacts cardiovascular health, showing that leucine supplementation increases atherosclerosis plaque progression in mice in an mTORC1-dependent manner[233](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[234](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) and that the leucine metabolite isovaleric acid accelerates the development of atrial fibrillation.[235](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) However, other groups report that leucine supplementation improves vascular function in young and aged mice.[236](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[237](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[238](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#)

When it comes to longevity, while leucine supplementation extends the chronological lifespan of yeast,[239](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) leucine supplementation does not extend mouse lifespan.[240](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Collectively, these results suggest that changes in leucine are unlikely to be responsible for the benefits of a PR diet.

#### BCAA: Isoleucine

Isoleucine has recently emerged as a key regulator of metabolism and aging. Dietary isoleucine levels are correlated with body mass index in humans,[73](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) and increased circulating isoleucine levels are associated with increased mortality risk.[210](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) In Western diet-fed mice, increasing dietary isoleucine promotes body weight and fat accretion, potentially because of lower energy expenditure, as well as glucose intolerance and insulin resistance.[213](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#)

In contrast, isoleucine restriction consistently improves metabolic health across multiple dietary contexts and age groups in mice.[66](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[73](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[173](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[213](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Isoleucine restriction extends the lifespan of both _Drosophila_ and mice, reduces frailty and increases healthspan in mice, and rejuvenates aged mice at the molecular level, modifying signaling through nutrient-sensing pathways.[66](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[165](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[172](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[173](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[190](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Additionally, isoleucine restriction induces substantial changes in the epigenome, including PTMs on histone tails.[166](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[241](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Isoleucine restriction also improves AD outcomes in male mice.[242](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Together, these studies show that isoleucine is an exceptionally powerful modifier of the hallmarks of PR, and reduced isoleucine almost certainly contributes to the benefits of a PR diet.

#### BCAA: Valine

Valine metabolism involves the conversion to succinyl-CoA, which can enter the TCA cycle and produce ATP. Restricting valine alone reduces body weight, improves metabolic health, reduces hepatic senescence, and extends the lifespan of male mice.[185](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) In female mice, valine restriction is protective of AD progression.[242](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Dysregulated valine catabolism, driven by 3-hydroxy-isobutyrate in diabetic rats, has been shown to contribute to their insulin resistance.[243](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Carp fish fed a diet reduced in valine show reduced weight gain,[244](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[245](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) confirming dietary valine’s impact on body composition across species.

Human plasma association analysis found that reduced circulating valine is associated with aging and moderate cognitive impairment,[246](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) suggesting that valine supplementation might be beneficial. However, valine supplementation worsens glucose tolerance and induces gut inflammation and hepatic lipid deposition, thereby associating high valine levels with negative health markers.[247](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[248](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[249](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Reduced levels of valine may contribute to the benefits of PR, but the role of valine in healthy aging may vary between species.

### Other EAAs

While isoleucine, valine, and methionine restriction are the main EAAs driving the benefits of PR, other EAAs may also impact aging. A 1978 report showed that tryptophan restriction extends the lifespan of rodents[250](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#); however, the failure of this finding to be reproduced in the 2000s brings this conclusion into question. Histidine restriction blunts weight gain in Western diet-fed mice without extending lifespan.[251](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Another group reported that dietary threonine restriction improves the metabolic health of mice through direct FGF21 induction.[183](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Similarly, phenylalanine administration induces insulin resistance and contributes to T2D progression in mouse models, suggesting its negative contribution to healthy aging.[252](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Lastly, it was shown that a lysine-restricted diet ameliorates obesity in mice.[253](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) These data taken together highlight ongoing research into how these canonically understudied EAAs contribute to the hallmarks of PR.

The data on EAAs are abundant in comparison to NEAAs, yet new evidence is emerging for other amino acids that may play a role in the response to PR. While BCAAs and EAAs have long been thought to be responsible for the benefits of PR, investigations into the role of NEAAs have muddied the water.

### NEAAs

Traditional classification of amino acids into essential and non-essential relies on an organism’s ability to synthesize an amino acid from glucose or other amino acids. While EAAs are required from the diet and restriction of EAAs is sufficient to promote healthy aging and mimic the effects of PR,[49](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[183](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) there is currently little evidence that restriction of any single NEAA broadly recapitulates PR. Indeed, there is comparatively little research on NEAAs, most likely because restricting NEAAs has been thought to be futile, as they can be synthesized as needed to meet demand. However, emerging evidence suggests that dietary NEAAs can impact specific aspects of aging. As summarized in [Table 3](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#tbl3), we will discuss the role of each dietary NEAA and if they show as much promise as the essentials.
