#### Alanine

Alanine is a key metabolic amino acid that serves as a preferred substrate for gluconeogenesis by the liver[269](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[270](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) and stimulates insulin secretion in pancreatic β cells.[271](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Despite this central role in glucose metabolism, very little is known about how dietary alanine influences aging or metabolic health.

With regard to metabolism, alanine supplementation blunts high-protein-induced weight gain in mice.[272](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Alanine administration robustly activates AMPK in mouse liver, thereby reducing blood glucose levels[273](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#); however, administration to diabetic rats had no effect on body weight or blood glucose levels.[274](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) In yeast, deletion of the alanine transaminase (ALT) gene leads to an accumulation of alanine and decreased chronological lifespan.[254](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) In humans, observational studies have found that low ALT levels are associated with an increased risk of frailty and mortality in middle age.[275](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[276](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[277](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Alanine declines with age in circulation and some tissues across diverse species, including humans, mice, and wasps.[118](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[278](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[279](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) While it is clear that alanine impacts metabolism, the question of whether altering dietary levels impacts aging remains unknown.

#### Arginine

Arginine is a conditionally essential amino acid in mammals, as early embryos and the young are unable to synthesize the amount of arginine that is required for growth.[280](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[281](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Arginine plays a key role in multiple biochemical processes, including detoxification through the urea cycle, generation of nitric oxide for cardiovascular function, and immune regulation,[282](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) and is metabolized into compounds such as nitric oxide, urea, citrulline, creatine, agmatine, and polyamines, including spermidine.[283](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[284](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Arginine regulates mTORC1 activity,[285](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) and arginine depletion _in vivo_ and _in vitro_ reduces mTORC1 activity,[286](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) similar to the effects of PR. A recent report found that plasma arginine levels increase with age,[257](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) suggesting that less arginine might be beneficial.

_Drosophila_ fed extra arginine have increased body weight, decreased lifespan, and increased levels of oxidative stress.[255](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Mice lacking nitric oxide synthase, which converts arginine to citrulline and releases nitric oxide, have increased arginine levels and increased body weight, diminished metabolic health, and increased lipids in circulation and the liver.[287](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[288](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Supplementation of arginine to aged rodents was detrimental to kidney function.[289](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[290](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Long-term arginine supplementation of human endothelial cells results in increased senescence, partially driven by increased mTORC1 activity.[291](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[292](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) While these data argue that arginine supplementation is detrimental to health, there are also studies reporting that arginine supplementation is beneficial. For example, obese Zucker diabetic fatty rats supplemented with arginine in drinking water exhibited reduced weight gain, improved glucose tolerance, and smaller white fat pads.[293](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[294](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[295](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) One clinical study found that dietary arginine supplementation during obesity reduces blood pressure, circulating glucose, and serum fatty acids; improves wound healing; and may improve cardiovascular health,[296](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) while a clinical trial in middle-aged obese patients with T2D found that supplementation of arginine in combination with CR and exercise exhibited significant fat loss that was not observed in the placebo group.[297](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Potentially, these varied results may be explained by arginine supplementation having different impacts in lean and obese subjects.

Increasing arginine catabolism through drug treatment is often used as a therapy for patients with cancer,[298](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) as some cancer cells lack the enzymes to directly synthesize arginine themselves.[299](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Arginine depletion is generally successful at inducing apoptosis in cancer cells, leaving healthy somatic cells unscathed.[300](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Arginine deprivation of human cells in cell culture media is sufficient to reduce senescence,[257](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) making it a potential target for aging. The impact of dietary arginine restriction on aging and aging-associated diseases in laboratory models and humans is still unknown.

#### Asparagine/aspartate

Cellular asparagine is metabolized into aspartate; current literature on dietary aspartate is minimal, while dietary asparagine studies have some exciting results for lifespan. These two amino acids serve as building blocks for glucose and protein synthesis and directly activate two important nutrient-sensing pathways: AMPK and mTORC1.[301](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[302](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#)

Clinical studies have revealed that upon aging, circulating asparagine levels are reduced.[303](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[304](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) In humans, a higher asparagine/aspartate ratio is associated with increased risk of diabetes, suggesting that dysfunction of asparagine/aspartate homeostasis can worsen metabolic health.[305](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Analysis of community-dwelling older frail individuals revealed elevated plasma asparagine, suggesting that increased asparagine levels may be a marker of frailty.[306](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#)

Removing asparagine from yeast media significantly extends chronological lifespan, most likely by inhibiting TOR.[258](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Drug-induced depletion of asparagine has been used in the clinic for cancer treatment for many years. In this context, asparaginase is administered intravenously or intramuscularly, resulting in the apoptosis of cancer cells that lack the enzymes to produce the amino acid themselves.[307](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) While the exact mechanism of asparaginase-induced apoptosis is unknown, it is believed to be coordinated through PR hallmarks: GCN2, mTORC1, and FGF21.[107](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[308](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#)

Using _Drosophila_ to investigate dietary asparagine supplementation, a group recently found that supplementation extends lifespan through improved intestinal stem cell maintenance.[260](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) However, studies in _C. elegans_ suggest that there may be a limit at which supplementation becomes detrimental: at 1 and 5 mM, supplementation extended lifespan, but 10 mM supplementation significantly reduced lifespan.[259](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Additional studies of dietary manipulation of asparagine in mammals are needed to clarify if supplementation or restriction is beneficial for healthy aging.

#### Cysteine

Cysteine is a sulfur-containing amino acid that can be directly metabolized from methionine. In fact, most dietary methionine restriction studies also require depletion of cysteine, or the benefits of methionine restriction on metabolic health are lost.[309](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[310](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#)

There are few studies investigating cysteine restriction or supplementation on lifespan. Cysteine accumulation may promote aging and tissue dysfunction through mitochondrial decline.[311](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) One report found that dietary cysteine deficiency improves metabolic health through increased energy expenditure by activating thermogenesis and beiging in rodents.[261](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Conversely, cysteine supplementation at <5 mM concentrations to _C. elegans_ significantly extends median lifespan by 8%–16%[259](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#); however, this observation has not been reported in other organisms. As cysteine can affect mitochondrial function and metabolic health, it is important that future studies determine whether cysteine depletion affects other hallmarks of PR.

Human association studies have linked high cysteine levels to obesity, cardiovascular diseases, and neurodegenerative diseases.[312](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[313](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[314](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Dietary restriction of cysteine in humans increased FGF21 and reduced body weight, such as that observed in rodent PR studies.[203](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) As dietary cysteine depletion is canonically done in conjunction with methionine restriction, we are just now beginning to understand how important this NEAA is in aging and thus remains an active area of research.

#### Glutamic acid/glutamate

Glutamate is the most abundant neurotransmitter in the brain and has a critical role in learning and memory.[315](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) In normal aging, glutamate levels increase in the brain, which can drive the development of neurodegenerative disorders.[316](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[317](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) The current literature has conflicting results on the impact of external glutamic acid/glutamate, as supplementation[104](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) and restriction[258](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) both extend yeast chronological lifespan. _C. elegans_ supplemented with 1 or 5 mM had significant lifespan extension; however, at 10 mM, the lifespan was significantly shortened, suggesting a dose-dependent effect of glutamate.[259](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Interestingly, the exceptionally long-lived naked mole rat species has higher circulating glutamate levels compared to the normal aging C57BL/6J mice.[318](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#)

#### Glutamine

Glutamine has an integral role in energy metabolism, entering the TCA cycle through α-ketoglutarate.[319](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Long-term glutamine deprivation in _Drosophila_ increases senescence and decreases lifespan, while supplementation of glutamine ameliorated the senescent phenotype observed with glutamine deprivation.[262](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) A different group found that senescent cells require extracellular glutamine to exert the SASP and that glutaminase inhibition induces senescent cell death.[320](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#)

There are a few studies investigating glutamine supplementation in humans. One trial reported that glutamine supplementation in six females reduced body weight without impacting other metabolic health parameters,[321](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) while another group reported that glutamine supplementation reduced waist circumference but not weight in overweight adults.[322](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Overall, the literature is not conclusive on whether supplementing or restricting glutamine is beneficial for aging.

#### Glycine

Glycine plays an important role in metabolism, serving as a substrate for glutathione, a key antioxidant scavenger.[323](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Glutathione levels are reduced in individuals with obesity, which could exacerbate the pro-inflammatory phenotype of the adipose tissue,[324](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) suggesting that increased glutathione could be a therapeutic strategy for age-associated obesity.

The ITP reported that glycine supplementation beginning at 9 months of age extended the lifespan of male and female mice by 6.2% and 3.7%, respectively.[263](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Glycine supplementation also extends the lifespan of _C. elegans_,[259](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[264](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) although, conversely, overexpression of the glycine catabolic enzyme _Gnmt_ increases median lifespan in _Drosophila_.[325](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Supplementation of the diet with collagen, which is rich in glycine, improves healthspan and increases the lifespan of _C. elegans_,[326](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[327](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) _Drosophila_,[328](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) and rodents.[329](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[330](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Dietary supplementation with amino acids in the ratio found in collagen—3:1:1 glycine:proline:hydroxyproline—promotes longevity in mice and reduces biological age in humans.[331](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#)

Supplementation of glycine is associated with increased production of glucagon-like peptide-1 (GLP-1), potentially because the cells that release GLP-1 are glycine-gated channels.[332](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) This phenomenon may explain the improvements in metabolic health seen with glycine supplementation. Higher glycine levels in human plasma are associated with improved metabolic health through improved insulin sensitivity,[333](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[334](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) and glycine supplementation improves metabolic dysfunction-associated fatty liver disease (MASLD) in laboratory models.[335](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) However, glycine supplementation does not impact body weight in individuals, suggesting the improved metabolic health is observed more at the pathological level and not at the physiological level.[336](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Interestingly, the mechanism responsible for the lifespan extension of glycine supplementation and the impact on human healthspan and lifespan are currently unknown.

#### Proline

Proline serves as the major nitrogen donor for synthesizing polyamines.[265](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Impairment in the proline catabolic pathway leads to shortened lifespan through impaired mitochondrial function, suggesting that proline may be beneficial for healthy aging.[337](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[338](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Proline levels in plasma, muscle, and skin are reduced in aged organisms,[339](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[340](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) and supplementation extends the lifespan of yeast and _C. elegans_.[259](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[265](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Proline levels are reduced in senescent cells, and supplementation decreases senescence of mesenchymal stem cells in culture.[266](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[341](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) It is hypothesized that proline may promote lifespan through its ability to scavenge ROS.[342](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#)

#### Serine

Serine contributes to diverse metabolic processes, with serine intermediates playing roles in glycolysis, methionine cycling, and lipid synthesis.[343](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Rodent models of obesity and insulin resistance have reduced tissue levels of serine, and supplementation with serine alleviates some of the co-morbidities that occur with diabetes.[344](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[345](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Normal aging in humans leads to lower circulating serine levels that contribute to age-related disorders.[346](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[347](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Mice fed a serine free diet or pharmacological depletion had increased hepatic lipid accumulation,[348](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) increased inflammation and oxidative damage,[349](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) suggesting that serine supplementation rather than depletion may be beneficial for healthy aging.

Studies in yeast[350](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) and _C_. _elegans_[259](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[267](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) reveal that dietary supplementation of serine leads to lifespan extension. Serine supplementation in rodents improves metabolic health, improving glycemic control and reducing hepatic lipid accumulation[351](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) while decreasing body weight and food intake in aging rodents.[352](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) These data taken together suggest that decreased serine is unlikely to drive the benefits of PR, as supplementation is beneficial for metabolic health and lifespan.

#### Tyrosine

Observational and genome-wide association studies (GWASs) have shown that dietary tyrosine negatively impacts lifespan[353](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) as well as insulin sensitivity.[354](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Serum tyrosine levels are associated with obesity, diabetes, liver dysfunction, and increased all-cause mortality in laboratory models and humans.[355](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#),[356](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Tyrosine levels increase with age in _Drosophila_[357](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) and humans,[358](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) and it is believed that this is due to the tyrosine degradation pathway being dysregulated upon aging.[357](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) Dietary depletion of tyrosine in female _Drosophila_ significantly extended lifespan, most likely through increased activation of the GCN2/ATF4 pathway.[268](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#)

In young individuals, short-term tyrosine supplementation enhances memory,[359](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) perhaps by increasing levels of tyrosine-derived neurotransmitters, but this is not true in older individuals.[358](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) However, more laboratory and clinical studies are needed to fully conclude the role of dietary tyrosine depletion and its impact on aging, especially outside of the context of cognitive function.

In summary, it is evident that the mounting research on NEAAs and their metabolites shows that not all NEAAs are the same. Dietary supplementation of some NEAAs may promote healthy aging, while dietary depletion of others may be beneficial for health and longevity. This evidence suggests that NEAAs may be just as important as the EAAs, and researchers must navigate how dietary NEAAs interact with cellular pathways to promote healthy aging.

## Discussion

As the world’s population grows ever older, it is becoming increasingly imperative to identify interventions that can promote healthy aging and reduce the burden of age-related disease. Over the past two decades, research has identified a critical role for dietary protein in the regulation of lifespan, with PR extending lifespan in _Drosophila_ and rodents. The subsequent discovery that restricting specific amino acids can recapitulate the benefits of PR has transformed our understanding of how diet regulates aging. Research has highlighted that there is a shared conserved response to aging-associated phenotypes between CR, PR, and amino acid restriction. Understanding the strings that connect diet, aging, and maintenance of organismal function could be imperative for advancing therapeutic interventions in aging.

In this review, we detail, for the first time, the hallmarks of PR: improved metabolic health, induction of nutrient-sensing pathways, decreased senescence, improved mitochondrial function, altered epigenome, and the promotion of healthy aging. Taken together, these hallmarks describe the robust impact of PR on aging-related phenotypes.[360](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) While these hallmarks are seen in most organisms on dietary PR, some variations exist based on biological sex or animal strain.[64](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) The hallmarks discussed in this review are highly interconnected, providing a firm foundation for future exploration on the causal nature of these hallmarks to understand which hallmarks are most important in orchestrating the response to PR. Undoubtedly, as research in the field continues, there will be additions to our proposed hallmark list that may shed further light on the mechanisms of PR and identify new therapeutic targets.

## Limitations

There remain many unknowns about the optimal levels of protein intake. While many individuals are likely eating substantially more than the RDA for protein and could benefit from protein or amino acid restriction, some individuals may have a greater demand for protein. These include pregnant women, growing children, individuals who are eating a restricted number of calories, and individuals recovering from injuries, and restricting protein or specific amino acids could potentially be harmful for these groups. Many elderly individuals are protein deficient due to factors such as decreased appetite, financial constraints, and social isolation,[361](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) and protein or amino acid restriction could move these individuals into a protein- or amino acid-insufficient state. Finally, those who are exercising may have higher protein needs or at least be able to consume higher levels of protein or specific amino acids without metabolic risk.

While challenges remain in determining the optimal levels of dietary protein and specific amino acids for individual health, the potential benefits of doing so are great enough that they demand that scientists take the necessary next steps to translate these interventions into therapies for humans. The next critical steps place an enormous amount of responsibility on clinical researchers. We must work together to design suitable diets for individuals; identify methods and metrics to assess the effects of diet on health, frailty, and aging; and understand how personal factors impact dietary needs.

Many questions remain about the role specific amino acids play in the lifespan-extending benefits of PR. Manipulating dietary NEAA levels could significantly impact the aging process of certain tissues and could promote metabolic health, brain function, and cardiovascular disease. One limitation of this conclusion is that most of the NEAA studies were done in lower-order organisms such as yeast or _C. elegans_,[259](https://www.cell.com/cell-press-blue/fulltext/S3051-3839\(26\)00077-0#) thus highlighting the need for more in-depth research in mammals to fully understand how the restriction of each dietary amino acid contributes to the PR phenotype and overall organismal aging. By targeting specific NEAAs, researchers could identify metabolic pathways to therapeutically target to improve health outcomes with aging.
